We introduce PartiGen, a part-aware latent diffusion framework that incorporates anatomical structure into a shared whole-body motion representation. Part-specific attention processes anatomically masked frame features, and its outputs are fused to combine local limb information with whole-body context. Conditioned on text and motion history, the model generates motion primitives autoregressively. Forward-kinematics supervision encourages kinematic consistency, while a learned end-of-sequence predictor selects a frame-level endpoint after generation. On HumanML3D retargeted to the Unitree G1, PartiGen achieves a segment-level FID of 3.165, a 16.8% reduction over TextOp, and improves R@1 by 1.4 percentage points. In the duration study with 16-frame primitives, endpoint prediction reduces mean absolute duration error from 12.471 to 3.080 frames compared with a fixed-length cutoff. We further evaluate robot execution using a pretrained, frozen GRIT tracker: 409 of 500 generated references (81.8%) pass the posture-based stability screen in MuJoCo. Following simulation screening and manual selection, 26 of 27 attempted motions are judged successful on the real G1.

### Paper [51]
Title: EndoRSI: Physical-Evidence-Driven Recursive Improvement of Endoscopic Robot Policies
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13376
Authors: Chi Kit Ng, Yidong Zhang, Tianchun Wu, Siu Hing Lui, Hanlin Zhao, Huxin Gao, Hongliang Ren
Abstract: Artificial intelligence is beginning to automate scientific discovery, yet physical robot-policy research still relies on scientists and engineers to interpret experimental failures and decide how controllers should be revised. We present EndoRSI (Endoscopic Recursive Self-Improvement), a human-governed, self-improving robotic research system in which the embedded EndoForge coding agent iteratively develops and refines endoscopic robot policies using evidence from physical experiments. Physical execution remains isolated within a safety-constrained runtime under human authorisation. In motorised endoscopy, validation evidence first caused the agent to reject an initial action representation, while subsequent physical experiments falsified an offline-supported insertion strategy by revealing hazardous closed-loop behaviour absent from the source data. Failed physical rollouts were then incorporated into policy revision; insertion success subsequently increased from 2/21 in the first generation to 17/21 in the fourth. The frozen final policy retained competence across visual and hardware shifts and was the best performing autonomous policy across six independent ex-vivo porcine specimens. These results show that physical robot interaction can serve as experimental evidence for agent-driven robot policy research.

### Paper [52]
Title: Robot-WATCHDOG : failure detection through object-centric graph representation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13403
Authors: Quentin Rolland, Fabrice Mayran de Chamisso, Jean-Baptiste Mouret
Abstract: Reliable real-time failure detection is critical for deploying learned robotic policies, yet defining failures explicitly is infeasible in open-world settings. This motivates failure detection from nominal demonstrations alone. Existing methods, however, struggle to balance robustness to benign variations with the ability to capture semantic and interaction-level errors. We introduce Robot-WATCHDOG, a self-supervised framework that models manipulation scenes as object-centric spatio-temporal graphs. Building on this representation, we investigate two complementary failure scores: Trajectory Projection and Correlation (TPC), a non-parametric measure of geometric deviation and relational consistency, and GnnT, a Graph Attention Network with a Transformer that predicts future object states. We evaluate both on a public benchmark and on a new dataset of semantically complex manipulation tasks, across six tasks and ten random seeds. Both scores outperform image-based failure detectors, particularly on semantic and interaction-level failures. TPC provides the strongest performance in AUPR and MCC on both dataset averages, with low variance across seeds. Qualitative results are made available at https://cea-list.github.io/Robot-WATCHDOG/.

### Paper [53]
Title: ManeuverFlow: Maneuver Hypothesis Generation with Future Evidence for Trajectory Evaluation and Refinement in End-to-End Autonomous Driving
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13409
Authors: Yuhao Wei, Yong Chen, Chen Yang, Yingchen Wang, Delin Ouyang, Lingfeng Qi, Qi Lan, Zhanhua Zhang, Jie Li, Guofa Li
Abstract: End-to-end planning in complex scenes must model diverse driving intentions and select the most appropriate trajectory using scene context and future risks. However, multi-query regression may collapse to one mode, while anchor-based and generative planners share a scaling bottleneck: small candidate sets limit maneuver coverage or discovery of high-quality trajectories, whereas large sets increase generation and scoring costs. We propose ManeuverFlow to generate trajectories for distinct intentions in one step and evaluate and refine them using current and future evidence. The model selects relevant maneuver modes, draws corresponding noise from the Gaussian mixture prior, and maps it to intention-specific trajectories through a maneuver-routed mixture-of-experts MeanFlow field. Current features and future features predicted by the world model are aggregated along each candidate path into trajectory-specific evidence for PDM-factor prediction. Using the same evidence, the refiner predicts local Frenet residuals for selected candidates; refined and retained candidates are then jointly re-scored for selection. With a lightweight TransFuser-style ResNet-34 backbone, ManeuverFlow achieves 90.6 PDMS and 90.1 EPDMS on NAVSIM v1/v2 navtest, respectively. On WorldEngine's recent 3DGS closed-loop benchmark, it attains the highest listed PDMS$^\ast$ scores of 75.19 and 74.58 under logged and IDM-controlled traffic, respectively. Code: https://github.com/wyh-autonomy/maneuverflow.

### Paper [54]
Title: Reflect-VLA: Internalizing Reflective Guidance via Dual-Path On-Policy Self-Distillation for Autonomous Driving
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13453
Authors: Yuechen Luo, He Chong, Fang Li, Shaoqing Xu, Hexin Zhang, Ruiqi Zhang, Ziying Song, Lei Yang, Zhi-Xin Yang, Fuxi Wen
Abstract: Reinforcement Learning (RL) substantially improves planning performance in autonomous-driving Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models. However, existing RL methods rely on scalar trajectory rewards that measure overall quality but fail to identify specific planning errors or provide actionable correction signals. In long-tail scenarios, errors in scene understanding, behavior selection, and trajectory execution are compressed into a single score. We observe that Reflective Guidance turns trajectory evaluation into explicit diagnoses and corrective instructions, substantially improving planning accuracy. Yet such guidance requires an initial prediction and its evaluation results, making it unavailable for direct single-pass inference. We propose Reflect-VLA, which treats Reflective Guidance as training-time privileged information and internalizes its planning benefits through Dual-Path On-Policy Self-Distillation (Dual-OPSD). Unlike scalar-reward optimization, Dual-OPSD uses the driving score only to route student-generated trajectories. It distills dense token-level corrective distributions from the teacher for risky trajectories while applying self-imitation to preserve reliable ones. To provide reliable corrective distributions on student rollouts, the teacher is progressively constructed through Driving-Knowledge Pretraining, Planning-and-Refinement SFT, and Self-Refinement Rejection Sampling. At inference time, Reflect-VLA generates trajectories in a single pass without explicit guidance or iterative refinement. Experiments on NAVSIM v1/v2 and Bench2Drive demonstrate competitive planning performance while reducing training time by over 90% compared with RL.

### Paper [55]
Title: RESCUE: Is This Alarm Worth Acting On? Counterfactual Evaluation of VLA Runtime Monitors
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13516
Authors: David Ahmedt-Aristizabal, Abdelwahed Khamis, Mohammad Ali Armin, Lars Petersson
Abstract: Runtime failure monitors for robot policies are typically evaluated by how accurately they distinguish failed from successful executions. Yet an accurate alarm may arrive too late for recovery, while an unnecessary intervention may disrupt an execution that would otherwise succeed. We introduce RESCUE, a counterfactual framework for evaluating whether a monitor's alarms enable useful interventions. By restoring simulator and policy states at execution checkpoints, RESCUE compares alternative actions, identifies the latest checkpoint satisfying a specified recovery criterion, and evaluates whether acting on alarms reduces expected task-completion cost. We evaluate RESCUE with OpenVLA-OFT and $\pi_{0.5}$ over LIBERO-Spatial and LIBERO-Plus using 128,340 counterfactual branches. Recoverability depends on the state--intervention pair rather than on robot state alone: at $\tau=0.5$, 14 of 66 OpenVLA-OFT/LIBERO-Plus failures contain a recoverable checkpoint under continuation, compared with 34 of 66 under continuation or scripted recovery, while recovery reduces recoverability in other conditions. On the primary strong-policy conditions, favourable detection scores do not establish lower intervention cost. Under the evaluated stop-and-reset model, even an idealised detector can afford only a 2.0--3.3% false-alarm rate before unnecessary resets outweigh the savings from stopping failures early. These results show that monitor value depends jointly on the intervention available and the workload in which it is deployed.

### Paper [56]
Title: Beyond Successful Robot Demonstrations: Learning from Human Interactions and Modeling Failure Dynamics
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13522
Authors: Peiyan Tu, Cong Wang, Hanxin Zhu, Xin Jin, Zhibo Chen
Abstract: Action-conditioned world models offer a promising foundation for generalist robotic intelligence by predicting the consequences of actions. However, existing robotic datasets provide limited coverage of the diverse interaction dynamics required for generalizable world modeling. Large-scale egocentric human videos offer complementary interaction experience, yet the embodiment gap between human hands and robotic manipulators hinders effective cross-embodiment learning. Meanwhile, the predominance of successful demonstrations leaves failure dynamics substantially underrepresented, limiting the ability of world models to anticipate unsuccessful executions. To address these limitations, we introduce HF-WM, an action-conditioned world model that combines Human interaction learning with Failure-aware post-training. To bridge the embodiment gap, we develop a dual-branch framework that couples video generation with auxiliary pointflow prediction, using stabilized pointflow as a unified motion representation across human and robot interactions while suppressing egocentric camera motion. To improve failure modeling, we further perform Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) post-training on perturbed actions derived from successful demonstrations, guided by action alignment and physical plausibility rewards, enabling the model to learn plausible failure dynamics without paired ground-truth videos. Extensive experiments demonstrate that HF-WM achieves state-of-the-art performance in action-conditioned video generation and exhibits strong utility for policy evaluation in downstream robotic tasks.

### Paper [57]
Title: ATHLETE: Learning Reactive Humanoid Tennis via Trajectory-Guided Motion Matching
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13572
Authors: Zhihao Tian, Zifan Wang, Enzheng Zhu, Xun Yang, Jialong Qin, Mengbo Wang, Junwei Liang
Abstract: Humanoid tennis requires reactive whole-body coordination under rapidly changing ball dynamics, yet collecting task-specific motion-capture demonstrations that cover diverse approach states, contact locations, and timing conditions is costly. Inspired by how humans acquire new skills by combining reusable motor experience with task-specific demonstrations, we present ATHLETE, a framework for learning reactive humanoid tennis from generic locomotion data and ordinary tennis videos. ATHLETE combines LAFAN1 locomotion with video-derived striking motions and uses Trajectory-Guided Motion Matching (TGMM) to construct diverse task-conditioned whole-body references. These references are transformed into a reactive policy through physics-based teacher--student learning with online DAgger distillation, followed by PPO fine-tuning for the tennis task. The resulting policy responds directly to robot and ball observations without requiring reference-motion inputs at deployment. On a real humanoid robot, ATHLETE achieves forehand and backhand hit rates of $90.00\%$ and $86.67\%$, respectively, and enables multi-shot rallies with human players. These results demonstrate that ATHLETE can learn dynamic humanoid tennis skills from reusable locomotion data and video demonstrations. Result videos and code can be found at https://anonymous.4open.science/w/ATHLETE2027/.

### Paper [58]
Title: RARA: Robust Policy Fine-Tuning through Retrieval-Anchored Representation Alignment for Robotic Manipulation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13578
Authors: Xuhui Kang, Sung-Wook Lee, Yan Zhuang, Tianle Zhong, Yen-Ling Kuo
Abstract: Pre-trained robot policies, such as Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models, provide a strong foundation for generalist robotic manipulation, but adapting them to new tasks with only a few demonstrations remains challenging. Directly fine-tuning a pre-trained policy often achieves strong in-distribution performance, yet it can distort the representations learned during pre-training and weaken the model's generalization ability under out-of-distribution shifts. We propose Retrieval-Anchored Representation Alignment (RARA), a two-stage adaptation method, to address this issue. First, RARA retrieves representation features from pretraining with similar task intent and motion, and aligns the policy's representations of the target demonstrations with the pre-trained representations of the retrieved demonstrations. Second, RARA fine-tunes the policy on the target demonstrations while limiting how much its representations of the retrieved demonstrations change during fine-tuning. By reducing the representation mismatch before action learning and preserving pre-trained knowledge during adaptation, RARA limits unnecessary representation drift during fine-tuning. Experiments with Diffusion Policy and GR00T on 13 tasks in RoboTwin, RoboCasa, and on a real robot show that RARA improves the average success of fine-tuning by 9.5 points in-distribution and 13.6 points out-of-distribution, and outperforms all compared baselines. Project page: https://shua-kang.github.io/RARA_robot/

### Paper [59]
Title: JitterVLA: A Replay Benchmark for Motion Quality in VLA-Driven Human-Robot Manipulation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13628
Authors: Faith Ogunwoye, Didar Seyidov, Konstantin Gubernatorov, Dzmitry Tsetserukou
Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) policies issue sparse targets whose arrival at the robot controller is delayed and irregular, so a reasonable predicted action can still produce abrupt motion near people. Remedies are evaluated on different streams, delays and metrics. JitterVLA replays identical commands and arrival traces through alternative controllers, isolating the controller's effect. We apply it to synthetic reaches, hand trajectories from 20 people, three open VLA pipelines (838 episodes) and a physical UR3. Interpolation lowered modeled p99 jerk for every source, by 83% (615 to 106 m/s^3) on human motion with 200 ms delay, at a 2.0 cm tracking cost, and the effect replicated on 598 new episodes; jerk-limited generation lowered jerk further at a larger cost. Entropy-discontinuity associations had opposite, replicated signs in two pipelines, and entropy gating helped in one but not the other. On the UR3, interpolation lowered accelerometer-derived jerk by 3.0-14.0% in all six exploratory pairs, while a position-derived metric did not resolve the contrast. JitterVLA supports screening before human-centred studies; code and data will be released.

### Paper [60]
Title: AutoBio+: Benchmarking Dexterous Manipulation with Dynamic Experimental Phenomena in Realistic Laboratories
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13643
Authors: Xuanye Wu, Yue Shi, Yongcan Yang, Yiqun Wang, Zhaoqi Wan, Dayu Xia, Jiezhang Cao, Xiangfei Li, Yao Mu, Guangtao Zhai
Abstract: Automating biological laboratories requires robots to perform dexterous tool interactions under realistic visual conditions while handling dynamic experimental phenomena, presenting significant challenges for robotic manipulation. To enable systematic evaluation under these conditions, we introduce AutoBio+, a simulation benchmark and framework for dexterous manipulation in realistic biological laboratory environments. AutoBio+ comprises 14 tasks across 4 robot embodiments, spanning functional tool use, precision manipulation, language-conditioned visual grounding, and instrument operation. We introduce controllable dynamic experimental phenomena, including flames, vapor, and bubbles, to capture the evolving visual conditions of laboratory procedures. To further enhance environmental realism, we develop a hybrid rendering pipeline that integrates interactive simulated foreground objects with Gaussian-splatting backgrounds reconstructed from 6 real laboratory scenes. For efficient large-scale data generation and evaluation, we further build a parallel simulation framework on top of mjlab, proposing Parallel Finite-State Machine (PFSM) and integrating batched rendering with decoupled trajectory replay for demonstration generation and model-independent closed-loop policy evaluation. Our evaluation reveals substantial variation in policy performance across tasks, exposing distinct strengths and weaknesses of current manipulation policies. Real-world experiments further demonstrate a reduced sim-to-real gap, supporting the validity of AutoBio+ for realistic manipulation evaluation. Supplementary materials are available at https://autobio-plus.github.io/AutoBioPlus.

### Paper [61]
Title: Cooperative Aerial Transportation of a Payload with a Sliding Cable Attachment
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13665
Authors: Sebastian Tilaguy Lezama, Diego S D'Antonio
Abstract: This paper presents a dual-quadrotor hook- suspended transport system for cooperative aerial manipulation of a payload that is not rigidly attached to a fixed point along the cable. Two quadrotors are connected by a single inextensible cable that passes through a frictionless hook mounted on the payload, allowing the cable to slide between its two segments. This introduces a cable-distribution variable that couples the quadrotor motion, cable geometry, and payload dynamics. A feasible reference-generation framework is formulated using the desired payload pose, cable distribution, and system shape while satisfying the cable-length constraint and elliptical workspace. The quadrotors track the geometrically generated trajectories while maintaining positive cable tension and respecting actuator limits. The payload pose is estimated from the system geometry, and its measured position is used only to validate the desired trajectory. The approach is evaluated through experimental demonstrations.

### Paper [62]
Title: Towards a Unified Taxonomy of Agricultural Harvesting Technologies: Linking Physical Interaction, Automation, and Selectivity
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13688
Authors: Renato Cristiano Torres, Fabiano Mariath D Oliveira, Claudio Gottschalg Duque
Abstract: Harvesting technologies have evolved from conventional mechanized systems based on bulk mechanical interaction toward increasingly automated and robotic systems capable of perception, decision-making, and localized actuation. This evolution has also created a classification problem, as harvesting systems are commonly described according to crop type, mechanical removal principle, sensing technology, robotic architecture, or degree of automation, making direct comparison difficult. This paper proposes a unified taxonomy for agricultural harvesting technologies based on two primary classification axes: the physical principle governing plant-machine interaction and the level of automation incorporated into the harvesting process. Selectivity is treated as an emergent functional attribute that expresses the biological and spatial scale at which perception and decision-making influence physical actuation. Conventional mechanized methods, including cutting, pulling, vibration, impact, brushing, pneumatic action, and combined systems, are analyzed together with semi-automated and robotic harvesting approaches. The analysis shows that increasing automation does not necessarily result in greater selectivity. A qualitative transition occurs when perception and decision-making directly influence product-level actuation, shifting harvesting from distributed or bulk interaction toward localized and selective manipulation. The proposed framework provides a common conceptual basis for positioning and comparing mechanized, automated, and robotic harvesting technologies. By separating the physical mechanism of product removal from the automation level and resulting selectivity, the taxonomy clarifies technological boundaries, identifies intermediate configurations, and supports the analysis and development of future harvesting systems.

### Paper [63]
Title: SmolWAM: A Latent World-Action Model for Robotic Manipulation on Edge Devices
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13740
Authors: Timi Owolabi, Hikmah Olawore, Frances Adelakun, Ayotomiwa Oyewumi
Abstract: World-action models (WAMs) predict a robot's actions together with the observations those actions will produce. However, the strongest models rely on video diffusion transformers that are far too computationally expensive for resource-constrained edge devices. We present SmolWAM, a latent WAM that we derive from the 2B-parameter Cosmos Policy by keeping its pretrained trunk and rebuilding its inference interface: single-frame visual latents, cached text features that preserve the trunk's attention sinks, 12 of 28 transformer blocks, and 1,008 tokens from which actions and future visual latents are read out in one rectified-flow step. Trained in 3.6 GPU-hours, SmolWAM succeeds in 95.5% of 400 LIBERO episodes at 57 ms and 2.3 TFLOP per 16-action chunk with 2.4 GiB of GPU memory, 11x faster than its teacher with 18.5x fewer FLOPs and 3.2x less memory, and its world head still forecasts scene motion.

### Paper [64]
Title: When LiDAR Fades, Radar Takes Over: LiDAR Reliability-Aware Adaptive Localization Under Airborne Obscurants
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13788
Authors: Moumita Mukherjee, Avijit Banerjee, Anton Koval, George Nikolakopoulo
Abstract: LiDAR-inertial odometry (LIO) is widely used for real-time localization and mapping, but its reliability can degrade substantially in subterranean environments affected by smoke, dust, and other airborne obscurants, whereas radar remains comparatively robust. This article presents a one-class temporal learning framework for LiDAR degradation detection and reliability-aware localization under such conditions. A broad set of degradation-sensitive cues, including spatial, cross-modal, geometric/motion, intensity, and measurement-recency features, is evaluated to characterize changes in LiDAR sensing quality. Their temporal evolution is modelled over sliding windows using an LSTM autoencoder trained exclusively on nominal operation, enabling deviations from healthy sensor behaviour to be detected without requiring labelled degradation data. The resulting LiDAR reliability estimate governs adaptive selection between LIO and radar inertial odometry (RIO), retaining LIO during reliable sensing and transferring localization authority to RIO when degradation is detected. The framework is evaluated using real-world ground robot data collected in GNSS-denied subterranean tunnels under nominal and dense-smoke conditions. Experimental results demonstrate degradation detection, adaptive modality switching, and continued localization output under severe LiDAR degradation. A feature family ablation study further shows that a reduced feature representation can improve degradation-aware localization performance in the evaluated dense-smoke scenarios, highlighting the importance of feature parsimony in one-class sensor degradation detection. The code will be released as open source to support reproducibility and further research.

### Paper [65]
Title: Dream to Remember: Continual Steering of Generalist Robot Policies with World Models
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13816
Authors: Bahey Tharwat, Iman Nematollahi, Marvin Alles, Xingyuan Zhang, Akshay L Chandra, Abhinav Valada
Abstract: Generalist robot policies are trained with imitation learning and are bounded by the quality and coverage of their demonstrations. Reinforcement learning can improve and extend them, either by fine-tuning the policy weights or by training a small module to steer the input noise of a frozen generative policy. When tasks arrive sequentially, both approaches suffer from catastrophic forgetting. Fine-tuning on a task degrades prior tasks, and steering only shifts forgetting to the shared steering module. We propose Dream to Remember (D2R), which continually steers a frozen generalist policy toward new tasks, while retaining its earlier skills. As the steering module learns a new task, a frozen pretrained world model rehearses prior tasks through imagined rollouts, anchoring the module to its earlier behavior without a replay buffer or new data. When the world model is faithful enough, D2R learns the new task entirely in imagination. Otherwise, it learns the task in the environment while using the world model only to rehearse prior tasks. On CALVIN, D2R improves eight tasks in sequence entirely in imagination, raising average success from 63.7% to 74.8%, while LoRA fine-tuning on the same sequence drops it to 11.7%. With a frozen off-the-shelf generalist policy and world model, $\pi_0$ and A2World, D2R learns unseen LIBERO-90 tasks in the environment while keeping $\pi_0$'s performance on the LIBERO suites it was fine-tuned on, even though the world model is not accurate enough for policy optimization. Finally, on a real Franka robot, D2R improves three tasks in sequence entirely in imagination while retaining the earlier improvements.

### Paper [66]
Title: MC-EvoBench: Auditing Experience-Driven Self-Improvement in Minecraft Agents
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13829
Authors: Jingwei Song, Zhisheng Chen, Bingfan Zeng, Jing Tang, Yubo Hou, Jinhao Jing, Zhengwei Xie, Linfeng Zhang
Abstract: A central challenge in embodied recursive self-improvement is enabling agents to convert interaction history into capabilities that persist beyond one world and transfer to later tasks. A saved memory or successful final action does not by itself establish that a reusable capability was acquired; evaluation must separate capability change from carried-over resources, unequal source exposure, and early execution failures. We construct MC-EvoBench as a controlled Minecraft benchmark with a fixed catalog of 19 registered evolution chains, including 12 primary evaluation chains and seven calibration or probe chains, together with three graph-aligned paired probe families for transfer. The benchmark represents each task as a task-state graph, defines stage-level environment verifiers, assigns matched source and target roles, resets the target world, and permits only a declared agent artifact to cross the source-target boundary. For each probe, no-prior, related, unrelated, and shuffled source conditions are evaluated under matched environment-step budgets and five seeds; we report TSR, WGP, and VMP and retain provenance records. The benchmark reveals a consistent direct-capability gap between controller and planner-backed variants, while related experience yields no stable transfer: it does not improve either tool probe, its small wood-species gain hinges on one seed, and all intervals include zero. Stage audits further show that early wood-collection and crafting bottlenecks often leave later capabilities unobserved. MC-EvoBench thus offers a controlled testbed for durable, transferable embodied capabilities. Code and data are available at https://github.com/xzw-ustc/mc-bench-open-source

### Paper [67]
Title: Mapless, Stabilizing, and Anti-Stuck Flipper Control for Articulated Tracked Robots
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13838
Authors: Valent\'yn \v{C}\'ihala, Pavel Sarga, Martin Pecka, Tom\'a\v{s} Svoboda, Karel Zimmermann
Abstract: Nearly every autonomous or semi-autonomous flipper controller assumes access to an elevation map of the terrain under and in front of the robot. The terrain beneath the chassis is occluded in the sensors, so the map is remembered through odometry integration, whose drift corrupts it. We present Stabilizing Anti-Stuck (SAS), a mapless controller that combines a stabilization term for body roll and pitch with an anti-stuck term that frees the robot when it gets stuck. As input, SAS uses the IMU, the flipper joint angles, and a motion cue that only tells whether the robot is moving. The cue is computed over a short window and never accumulated, so SAS does not inherit the odometry-integration drift that corrupts the map. We deploy SAS on a tracked robot with four flippers and evaluate it against manual teleoperation and prior flipper controllers in a large comparison, both in simulation and on a real obstacle. In simulation and on the real robot, SAS achieves traversal quality comparable to state-of-the-art map-based controllers. On a log hidden in tall grass, invisible to the LiDAR, the best map-based controller gets stuck while SAS traverses the hidden obstacle without a problem.

### Paper [68]
Title: HorizonNav: Geometry-Guided Video Diffusion for Anticipatory 3D Robot Navigation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13849
Authors: Hajira Amjad, Valerii Serpiva, Viacheslav Liubimov, Didar Seyidov, Dzmitry Tsetserukou
Abstract: We present HorizonNav, a video diffusion-based framework for anticipatory robot navigation that transforms a single RGB image and a text prompt into a geometrically aligned representation of plausible surrounding 3D space. Given the initial observation and a textual description of the reconstruction objective, a vision-language model, guided by designed instructions, interprets the scene and constructs structured prompts for a video diffusion model. The resulting video sequence provides plausible future observations of the environment, which are subsequently processed using monocular ORB-SLAM3 for camera pose estimation and Video Depth Anything for dense depth prediction. Our central contribution is a geometry-guided depth alignment stage that combines the estimated camera poses with triangulated visual landmarks to constrain dense depth predictions across multiple views. This alignment improves the geometric consistency of the reconstructed 3D environment, addressing inconsistencies that temporal coherence of the generated video alone cannot resolve. The experimental evaluation of navigation in imagined 3D environment shows that across a 19-scene horizon study, planning-feasible scenes increase from 13/19 at P20 to 15/19 at P81, while successful RRT trials increase from 62/90 to 75/90. In a separate corridor study, 6 of 25 evaluable generated-camera trajectories violate the reconstructed collision constraints; RRT planner recovers feasible alternatives in 5 of those 6 cases. A larger archive contains 58 usable reconstructions from 67 attempted cases, of which 36 support a downstream plan and 22 do not, exposing reconstruction and free-space failure modes rather than hiding them without lidar. Across 15 corridor reconstructions, the mean reconstruction error is approximately 0.30 m at matched structural landmarks.

### Paper [69]
Title: BT-Agent: Deploying Onboard Robot Agents with Behavior Trees and Multi-Fidelity Evaluation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13862
Authors: Fabio H\"ubel, Adam Lilja, Yue Yin, Siming He, Claire Tomlin, Shankar Sastry, Jonas Frey, Marco Pavone
Abstract: Deploying language-model agents onboard mobile robots requires task reasoning and responsive execution within limited compute and memory. We propose BT-Agent, an onboard architecture combining LLM-driven task decisions with predefined behavior-tree (BT) skills. These skills enforce execution conditions and return structured failure feedback. We demonstrate fully onboard operation with a 9B model on a quadruped across five task categories. Intervention tests show that local checks stop ongoing navigation without waiting for another model decision, while the agent remains active to retry or report constraints. To compare agent designs efficiently, we introduce a 161-task multi-fidelity benchmark, evaluated in text on all tasks and in Habitat on 39. Text supports 10.9$\times$ higher evaluation throughput, while simulation reveals how perception and motion failures change task outcomes. BT-Agent's largest success gains over the strongest Act/ReAct baseline occur at 4B and 9B: approximately 10 and 5 percentage points. Ablations identify structured tool calls and informative failure feedback as the most useful components.

### Paper [70]
Title: Chronos Policy: Learning Robot Policies on Physical Time
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13929
Authors: Changhao Wang, Jaden Clark, Shuran Song
Abstract: Robot policies often convert sensing and control into fixed-rate observation-action sequences, leaving physical time implicit in sequence position. This assumption breaks when observation rates or delays vary, or when inference latency changes. We introduce Chronos Policy, which makes physical time explicit by conditioning on observation capture times and desired action execution times. We study two ways to incorporate time into a diffusion transformer: adding timestamp embeddings to token features, or encoding physical time in positional encoding through time-aware rotary positional encodings (time-aware RoPE). We find that time-aware RoPE is more robust, allowing attention to depend directly on physical time while keeping token content unchanged. During training, we temporally randomize which observations are sampled and when the action chunk begins, exposing the policy to a wider range of sensing and execution schedules that may occur during deployments. This also enables learning from asynchronous, multi-rate data without forcing all measurements onto a fixed temporal grid. Across simulation and real-world experiments, Chronos Policy improves performance on dynamic tasks, where observation-to-action timing is critical, and on multisensory tasks, where the relative timing between observations matters. On standard quasi-static tasks, it preserves nominal performance while improving robustness to changes in sensing and inference timing.

### Paper [71]
Title: Noise Space Learning for VLA Adaptation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13943
Authors: Junjie Lu, Chuheng Zhang, Yucen Wang, Yuhua Jiang, Xinyao Qin, Min Xu, Li Zhao
Abstract: Pretrained vision-language-action (VLA) models encode broad manipulation priors, yet adapting them to specific tasks requires balancing limited robot interaction, computational resources, and safety constraints. Noise space learning (NSL) adapts flow-matching-based VLAs by learning a context-conditioned distribution over noise while keeping the pretrained VLA fixed. Building on prior noise-steering methods, we formulate NSL as an interface for VLA adaptation that accommodates different learning algorithms and instantiate it with PPO, SAC, and AWR. Theoretically, we provide an information-theoretic analysis of how NSL enables efficient adaptation. Empirically, we study these NSL algorithms on LIBERO, RoboTwin, and four real-robot manipulation tasks under interaction, computation, and execution constraints. Across these settings, we develop a suite of effective NSL variants and derive practical recommendations for choosing among them under different resource and deployment regimes.

### Paper [72]
Title: Splat2Act: From Captured Scenes to Personalized VLA Manipulation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13945
Authors: Xiaomeng Ye, Krishna Jaganathan, Gyubeum Lim, Gunjan Chhablani, Muhammad Zubair Irshad, Zsolt Kira
Abstract: A user who brings a generalist manipulation policy into their home or office wants it to pick up their objects from their shelves, counters, and tables. Yet even a strong generalist like $\pi_{0.5}$ achieves 0% pickup success in our captured deployment scenes without adaptation, and collecting real demonstrations in every user's environment is prohibitively expensive. We present Splat2Act, an automated real-to-sim-to-real pipeline that personalizes a pretrained vision-language-action (VLA) policy to a specific scene and object set using only a handheld phone scan and images of the user's objects. Unlike prior real-to-sim work that reconstructs individual tabletops, Splat2Act operates on building-scale indoor environments: it builds a photorealistic digital twin with 3D Gaussian Splatting and collision geometry, automatically discovers tabletop and non-tabletop receptacles, constructs feasible pickup tasks, and generates successful demonstrations with a privileged simulation oracle, with only scene and object capture required from a human. Applied to 101 scanned campus environments with a median navigable area of 104.87 m$^2$ and 7 receptacles, the pipeline yields 852 receptacles and over 68,000 pickup demonstrations. We fine-tune $\pi_{0.5}$ in two stages: Stage 1 adapts to hundreds of public object assets, drawn either from the deployment scene alone or from all 101 scenes; Stage 2 personalizes to the user's own objects on the deployment scene's receptacles. Our strongest personalized policy reaches 62.5% aggregate pickup success in the target scene. Deployed on a real Stretch 3, the same policy achieves 63.9% success on a 12-task subset, closely tracking its 66.7% simulated success on those tasks. These results suggest that automated real-to-sim-to-real training can personalize VLA manipulation policies using only simulation-oracle data.

### Paper [73]
Title: Imagine the Future, Internalize the Gist: Efficient VLA Reasoning via Internalized Spatiotemporal Imagination
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02626
Authors: Shenglan Li, Zhendong Mi, Hengyi Zhu, Jingwu Luo, Chun Kit Chan, Geng Yuan, Yanzhi Wang, Pu Zhao, Shaoyi Huang
Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models increasingly incorporate intermediate reasoning to improve robotic manipulation, yet existing approaches primarily reason about observed states without explicitly anticipating future scene evolution. Extending such reasoning to explicit future rollouts at every inference step, however, introduces substantial computational overhead. We propose IG-VLA, a VLA reasoning framework that enables models to imagine the future and internalize the gist. Our Latent Spatiotemporal Reasoning learns to imagine task-relevant future scene evolution directly in visual representation space, guiding action prediction without costly pixel-level video generation. To further reduce inference overhead, we introduce Scene Gist Memory, which internalizes reasoning-derived scene-behavior associations into a compact Scene Gist Token, preserving the benefits of future reasoning while bypassing explicit future imagination at inference. Extensive experiments on LIBERO, LIBERO-Plus, and VLABench demonstrate the effectiveness and efficiency of IG-VLA. On the LIBERO-Plus Language suite, both the reasoning and gist policies outperform the strongest baseline by nearly 6% in success rate. The gist policy also achieves up to 6.38x speedup over baselines, reducing inference latency from 1081ms to 169.5ms per action chunk on a single NVIDIA A6000 GPU. These results demonstrate that future spatiotemporal reasoning can be effectively internalized for efficient VLA deployment.

### Paper [74]
Title: Mind the Temporal Gap: Evaluating Video Models for Temporal Understanding in Driving
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12575
Authors: Shiva Sreeram, Erfan Aasi, Tsun-Hsuan Wang, Alaa Maalouf, Daniela Rus
Abstract: Modern video-language models are increasingly treated as general-purpose video reasoners, yet it remains unclear whether they robustly capture temporal dynamics in safety-critical settings such as driving. We study this question through controlled ego-motion tasks that require identifying actions from driving videos. Across video-language models, we find that standard prompting, visual prompting, few-shot prompting, and chain-of-thought prompting remain unreliable and highly task- and model-dependent, with persistent class biases, although newer models show substantial task-specific few-shot gains. In contrast, lightweight LoRA finetuning and simple probes trained on intermediate visual features recover strong motion-discriminative performance, indicating that motion-relevant cues are often present inside the models but are not reliably used by the final video-to-language pathway. These results suggest that, in driving, current video-language models remain unreliable temporal reasoners and often behave more like frame-level, bias-driven recognizers than dependable temporal world models.

### Paper [75]
Title: Distributionally Robust Adaptive Iterative Covariance Steering for Small-Body Proximity Operations
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12774
Authors: Vivek Khatana, Aditya Gahlawat, Naira Hovakimyan, Petros Voulgaris
Abstract: Autonomous small-body proximity operations require continuous-thrust guidance under uncertain gravity, solar-radiation pressure (SRP), and stochastic disturbances. We combine iterative covariance steering (iCS) with $L_1$ distributionally robust adaptive control ($L_1$-DRAC) to provide risk-aware collision avoidance despite mismatch between the nominal planning model and the true dynamics. Iterative covariance steering plans a nominal feedback policy satisfying distributionally robust conditional value-at-risk keep-out constraints, while $L_1$-DRAC compensates for dynamics uncertainty and bounds the residual distributional mismatch through a Wasserstein radius used by the planner. In $\mathbf{1000}$ paired Monte Carlo trials with gravity, SRP, and diffusion mismatch, $L_1$-DRAC adaptive control reduces the keep-out violation rate from $\mathbf{32.7\%}$ to $\mathbf{0.2\%}$ and the physical collision rate from $\mathbf{16.4\%}$ to zero, without actuator saturation.

### Paper [76]
Title: Reinforcement Learning via Fixed-point Flow Matching
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12848
Authors: Denis Blessing, Onur Celik, Gerhard Neumann
Abstract: In maximum entropy and behavior-constrained reinforcement learning (RL), the optimal policy is given by a Boltzmann distribution. Generating samples from this distribution can be challenging, as it is often complex and multimodal. While flow policies trained with flow matching provide a powerful framework for representing such distributions, they traditionally rely on access to training data, which is generally unavailable in online RL. To address this limitation, we propose fixed-point flow matching (FFM), a data-free procedure for training and fine-tuning flow policies in RL. FFM leverages the target flow identity to formulate the actor update as a fixed-point iteration, utilizing a flow-matching-style regression objective where the critic's action gradients serve as regression targets. We show that the optimal policy is a fixed point of this iteration. Through extensive experiments, we demonstrate that FFM effectively fine-tunes pretrained flow policies in the offline-to-online setting and enables efficient and accurate training of flow policies from scratch in online RL. These results establish fixed-point flow matching as a principled approach for learning expressive flow-based policies without requiring training data.

### Paper [77]
Title: What Visual Representations Do World Action Models Need?
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13024
Authors: Kai Liu, Jiangran Lyu, Tingyu Deng, Yixin Zheng, Jingtian Wan, Wenbo Cui, Mi Yan, Yizhou Zhou, Haoran Li, Dongbin Zhao, Zhizheng Zhang, He Wang
Abstract: World Action Models (WAMs) inherit powerful priors from pretrained video generation models, but their variational autoencoder (VAE) representations are optimized for visual reconstruction rather than manipulation. External visual representations can improve manipulation, but existing methods couple representation choices with different integration mechanisms, obscuring their individual effects. We systematically compare visual representations, five integration mechanisms, and feature combinations under controlled settings. Our study yields three main findings. First, strong general-purpose visual representations provide broader robustness gains and can match or outperform representations pretrained for specific visual capabilities. Second, channel-wise joint prediction achieves the highest average success rate; further analyses suggest that future-feature prediction and the action expert's effective use of added visual information contribute to its gains. Third, additional sources should be selected for the task-relevant information they contribute beyond existing representations, as the same source can help one combination but harm another. However, even the best evaluated composition provides a smaller additional gain than the initial improvement from adding a strong general-purpose representation to the VAE-only baseline. We instantiate the resulting Representation Recipe as R$^2$-WAM. It achieves state-of-the-art performance on RoboCasa GR1 Tabletop with an 84.7% success rate and reaches 92.8% on RoboTwin 2.0, improving over the VAE-only baselines by 5.9 and 2.2 percentage points, respectively. Taken together, these findings suggest that future work should prioritize stronger general-purpose visual representations and more effective mechanisms for integrating them into WAMs, with feature composition serving as a supplementary route to improvement.

### Paper [78]
Title: Sonic-AMG: Streaming Human Motion Generation from Spatial Audio
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13300
Authors: Jingcheng Ni, Zekun Li, Zhiyang Dou, Xiaoyan Cong, Ziyao Zeng, Hongyang Du, Randall Balestriero, Taku Komura, Srinath Sridhar
Abstract: Modeling human reactions to spatial audio requires understanding both what is heard and where it comes from. Prior work generates motion offline from complete acoustic and spatial input sequences. Humans, however, react to sounds without knowing what they will hear next. We present Sonic-AMG, to our knowledge the first framework for streaming human motion generation from spatial audio. The model encodes acoustic content and body-relative source geometry as separate tokens. Conditioned on these tokens, a causal transformer with a flow head generates continuous motion latents, from which a causal decoder reconstructs motion segments. Standard teacher-forced training derives motion histories and body-relative source locations from ground-truth motion, whereas streaming inference relies on generated motion for both, resulting in a training-inference mismatch. To reduce this history discrepancy, we introduce rehearsal training, which combines full-sequence teacher-forced supervision with supervised prediction along short autoregressive self-rollouts. To keep spatial conditioning consistent with generated motion, we propose spatial feedback, which recomputes body-relative source locations from generated root poses during both rehearsal training and inference. On SAM, Sonic-AMG achieves state-of-the-art FID and R-precision. It further supports real-time online generation of coherent reactions across sound events and source locations. Project page: https://jingchengni.com/sonic-amg/.

### Paper [79]
Title: NovaSplat: Model-Free 6D Object Pose Estimation via 3D Gaussian Splatting Onboarding and Refinement
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13309
Authors: Georgios Floros, Evangelos Sartinas, Orestis Vangelis, Maria Pateraki
Abstract: 6D pose estimation of novel objects without CAD models remains an open challenge: model-free methods must build the object representation from scratch during onboarding and have long trailed their model-based counterparts. We present NovaSplat, a model-free pipeline that closes this gap. From a short smartphone video captured at onboarding, we train a 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) model that serves both as a template source for detection and coarse pose estimation and as a differentiable backbone for pose refinement. Our refiner optimizes the object-to-camera pose with combined photometric and geometric losses under a continuous rotation parametrization free of gradient singularities. On a household-object benchmark, NovaSplat surpasses all model-based methods. On a challenging industrial benchmark with textureless and specular surfaces, it outperforms all model-free competitors while substantially narrowing the gap to model-based ones. We further introduce NovaSplat-Lite, an inference variant that is $3.1\times$ faster at negligible accuracy cost. Code will be made available at \href{https://github.com/POSE-Lab/novasplat}{https://github.com/POSE-Lab/novasplat}.

### Paper [80]
Title: AlphaNIST: Emergent Assembly Skills from Binary Task Rewards
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13354
Authors: Octi Zhang, Iretiayo Akinola, Rosario Scalise, Mateo Guaman Castro, Patrick Yin, Tobias Widmer, Miles Macklin, Chris Amevor, Dylan Turpin, Dirk Van Gelder, Jan Carius, Ankur Handa, Gavriel State, Yunrong Guo, Yashraj Narang
Abstract: The NIST Assembly Task Board #1 remains a challenging benchmark for learning-based methods in robotic manipulation. Over short horizons, it demands precise, contact-rich control of small, tight-fitting parts. Over long horizons, it requires recovery and coordination across extended action sequences. Demonstrations, task decomposition, and dense progress rewards have enabled research advances by providing guidance on how to assemble. But how much of the strategy must be specified? The progression from AlphaGo to AlphaGo Zero showed that RL at scale can discover superhuman strategies without prescribing how to win. AlphaNIST asks whether a sparse binary task reward and task diversity admitted by this task-agnostic reward can similarly let a robot discover how to assemble. In simulation, we train a single policy from scratch across all 20 assembly tasks on the NIST Assembly Task Board #1, reaching 98.2% overall training success without demonstrations, progress shaping, scripted primitives, or per-task tuning. The framework also trains policies to complete ten assembly pairs in a single episode without a prescribed order, with task success rewarded only when all ten pairs are simultaneously complete. The learned policies discover dexterous and planning-like behaviors: choosing grasps for later assembly, using surrounding geometry to reorient parts, and preparing one part, assembling another, then returning to finish the first without an explicit planner. For videos, see our website: https://alpha-nist.github.io/

### Paper [81]
Title: A Local Agentic AI Framework to Generate Edge Case Scenarios for Safety Assurance
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13479
Authors: Victoria J. Hodge, Colin Paterson, Rob Alexander
Abstract: Generating edge case scenarios is crucial for training, testing and safety assuring autonomous systems. This identifies hazardous failures to increase system reliability and robustness, ensures the system can still function safely on the boundaries of its operational design domain and learns the expected system behaviour for these edge cases. The rapid advancement in the generation and analysis capabilities of language models (LMs) has expanded their applicability and recent work has investigated LM scenario generation. However, many recent approaches rely on human-in-the-loop or rule-based strategies. This restricts the LM's ability to generate diverse and edge case scenarios; the long tail of the distribution. Our work uses local LMs in a laptop-based agentic AI framework for adaptive scenario generation. This is cheap, fully-controlled, ensures data privacy and security. It uses think, act, observe, update, and continue loops with lightweight JSON encoding to generate scenarios. We use the system requirements, system description and environment description as knowledge bases in contextual prompts for an LM to ensure scenario realism. A second, independent LM critiques each scenario using domain specific criteria so it can be refined. The refined scenarios are run in simulation and evaluation metrics collected. These metric scores are analysed and similar scenarios are created for edge cases (exploitation) or novel scenarios are generated to explore the scenario space. Post-hoc clustering using metric scores and thematic-type analysis of the rich-text scenario descriptions produced by the LM pinpoints functional gaps and their triggering conditions. This creates traceable evidence for the safety assurance case. Our work highlights the potential of a flexible and adaptive local agentic AI framework for automating edge case generation and producing traceable safety assurance evidence.

### Paper [82]
Title: MAGMA-Bench: Evaluating Long-Horizon Embodied Agents under Dynamic Tasks and Execution Failures
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13534
Authors: Loan Bernat (LAAS-GEPETTO), Matthieu Grard (LAAS-RAP), Ariane Herbulot (LAAS-RAP), Florent Lamiraux (LAAS-GEPETTO)
Abstract: Long-horizon embodied agents must maintain task-relevant information, coordinate heterogeneous robots, and adapt their decisions as execution unfolds. Existing benchmarks investigate dynamic task execution and embodied multi-agent coordination, but provide limited evaluation of their intersection. We introduce MAGMA-Bench, an architecture-agnostic benchmark for persistent task reasoning in dynamic multi-robot environments. MAGMA-Bench evaluates agents across increasing task horizons and controlled online perturbations, including execution failures, task interruptions, and mission updates, with scenarios requiring coordination across robots with heterogeneous capabilities. It uses a structured skill-level interface to separate task-level reasoning from perception and low-level control. We evaluate locally deployable open-weight planners, multi-agent state-tracking strategies, and frontier models. Even the strongest evaluated models fail on a substantial fraction of clean episodes, and their success rates decrease further under online perturbations. Supervised adaptation greatly improves a small model, but performance remains substantially lower on longer and multi-robot missions. We release the benchmark, 261,646 task-level training examples, and an architecture-agnostic expert-trace generation pipeline.

### Paper [83]
Title: Probabilistic Ship Trajectory Forecasting using Transformer Models on AIS Data
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13557
Authors: Frederik E. T. Sch\"oller
Abstract: Anticipation of vessel trajectories is an essential task when navigating at sea. This applies to both human navigators and autonomous surface vessels. Trajectory forecasting enables sub-routines such as collision avoidance to assess the situation and react accordingly in time. This paper investigates Transformer and Performer models for trajectory prediction of surface vessels in confined waters. Using widely available position data from the Automatic Identification System (AIS), candidate models are trained and evaluated. Historical information on how other vessels have moved in the local area surrounding each agent is conveyed using positional heat maps. The heat maps are generated using Kernel Density Estimation (KDE) and encoded with a Convolutional Neural Network (CNN) before entering the Transformer models. The models are trained with quantile loss to efficiently obtain a model-aware prediction interval, as this is crucial for timely collision avoidance. The proposed model architectures are shown to greatly outperform previous work while the inclusion of local heat maps enables the prediction of vessel movement that is not otherwise indicated from the previous positions.

### Paper [84]
Title: LAPT-Scale: Scaling Embodied Policies with Latent Action Pre-Training on Human Videos
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13732
Authors: Zhiyuan Xiang, Peidong Liu, Mingyang Li, Jiawei Li, Wenhow Li, Wenbo Ding, Xiang Deng
Abstract: Scaling generalist robotic policies is limited by the high cost of collecting embodied interaction data. Large-scale egocentric human videos offer a rich alternative, but they lack robot action labels, and existing action annotation methods are often noisy under real-world visual complexity and diverse manipulation dynamics. We introduce LAPT-Scale, a scalable latent action pre-training paradigm with two components: LAPT-Scale-LAM learns continuous latent actions by explaining frame-to-frame visual transitions in human videos and uses them as human-derived supervision, while LAPT-Scale-Policy trains a VLA policy through human-video latent-action pre-training, target-robot embodiment alignment, and task-specific post-training. Unlike prior vector-quantized LAMs, LAPT-Scale-LAM uses a continuous latent space to preserve fine-grained motion and interaction dynamics. We further combine perceptual, semantic, and optical-flow alignment signals to improve transition-level supervision beyond pixel reconstruction. Trained on 23.3K hours of egocentric video, LAPT-Scale-LAM provides implicit action supervision for policy pre-training, yielding consistent scaling gains and improving real-robot success from 63.3% to 85.0% as annotated human video scales from 1K to 5K hours. The learned policy also generalizes to unseen visual conditions.

### Paper [85]
Title: MARGoS: A Configuration-Driven Platform for Reproducible MARL Experiments in ARGoS
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13772
Authors: Tom Philipp Backert, Mohammad D. Soorati, Javad Ghofrani
Abstract: Multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) has become a prominent research direction, particularly within collective robotics, where the ARGoS simulator is widely used to study emergent swarm behavior. Yet despite this growth, systematic support for reuse and reproducibility in MARL-based swarm robotics remains limited and insufficiently standardized: a single experiment is assembled from manually aligned simulator files, training scripts, configuration values, paths, seeds, and environment details, so reuse and reproduction stay fragile even within one research group. To address this gap, we introduce MARGoS, a lightweight, configuration-driven platform developed in this project that elevates these experiment-defining inputs to first-class, platform-managed artifacts. MARGoS enables reproducible experimentation through configuration hashing, end-to-end seed propagation, frozen (immutable) configurations, structured logging, environment fingerprinting, and exportable experiment bundles that facilitate sharing, replay, and verification of results across machines and over time. A five-dimension evaluation (architecture, efficiency, reproducibility, usability, and collaboration) supports our claim that managing experiment variation as a platform responsibility makes ARGoS+MARL experiments easier to reuse, reproduce, and hand off. Our code is available at https://github.com/tombackert/margos.

### Paper [86]
Title: Tracking the Invisible: Self-Supervised Wheel Track Detection with Thermal-RGB Fusion
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13872
Authors: Yiming Yang, Jeremy P. Bos
Abstract: Accurate detection of drivable area is essential for autonomous driving and advanced driver assistance systems. While state-of-the-art methods focus on general traversability estimation, they prove inadequate for snow-covered roads where following existing wheel tracks offers crucial advantages: improved traction, predictable vehicle dynamics, and verified path stability. To address this, we propose a low-cost, self-supervised approach detecting safety-critical wheel tracks using synchronized RGB-I (RGB + Long-Wave Infrared) imagery. Our method automatically generates training labels by projecting future wheel trajectories from recorded GNSS data. A neural network is designed to identify wheel tracks with the 4-channel RGB-I images from challenging, white-out road surfaces. Experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach in visually monotonous and featureless adverse environment, offering a robust and cost-effective alternative for winter autonomy. The newly introduced ThermalTrack dataset is available at: https://digitalcommons.mtu.edu/thermaltrack/.

### Paper [87]
Title: Beyond the Snowflakes: Disentangling Weather Effects from Confounding Meta-Factors in LiDAR Object Detection
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13891
Authors: Yiming Yang, Jeremy P. Bos
Abstract: Light Detection and Ranging (LiDAR) is critical for autonomous perception, yet its performance is known to degrade in adverse weather. While existing literature models snow as atmospheric noise that degrades performance, our granular evaluation on the WADS-3D and CADC datasets reveals a counter-intuitive phenomenon: detection accuracy consistently improves as snow intensity increases. Through ablation study and meta-information analysis, we reconcile this by confirming that while falling snow acts as noise, its negative impact is outweighed by a dominant, concurrent factor: significantly increased object point count. We hypothesize that this object point count boost acts as a geometric enhancement, driven by snow accumulation increasing vehicle surface area and a shift from specular to diffuse reflectance. Our findings demonstrate that evaluating perception systems using broad weather-severity bins without accounting for correlated object-level data can lead to misleading conclusions about detector robustness. We conclude that accurate evaluation requires decoupling atmospheric noise from surface accumulation effects and outline future research to validate these physical models. The WADS-3D dataset is available at: https://digitalcommons.mtu.edu/wads-3d/.

### Paper [88]
Title: Development of a Low-Cost Prosthetic Hand Using Electromyography and Machine Learning
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.15533
Authors: Mosab Diab, Ashraf Mohammed, Yinlai Jiang
Abstract: Electromyography (EMG) is a measure of muscular electrical activity and is used in many clinical/biomedical disciplines and modern human computer interaction. Myo-electric prosthetics analyze and classify the electrical signals recorded from the residual limb. The classified output is then used to control the position of motors in a robotic hand and a movement is produced. The aim of this project is to develop a low-cost and effective myo-electric prosthetic hand that would meet the needs of amputees in developing countries. The proposed prosthetic hand should be able to accurately classify five different patterns (gestures) using EMG recordings from three muscles and control a robotic hand accordingly. The robotic hand is composed of two servo motors allowing for two degrees of freedom. After establishing an efficient signal acquisition and amplification system, EMG signals were thoroughly analyzed in the frequency and time domains. Features were extracted from both domains and a shallow neural network was trained on the two sets of data. Results yielded an average classification accuracy of 94.8% and 93.2% for the time and frequency domains respectively. Furthermore, results showed a faster computation and response for the time domain analysis; hence, it was adopted for the classification system. A wrist rotation mechanism was designed and tested to add significant functionality to the prosthetic. The mechanism is controlled by two of the five gestures, one for each direction. Which added a third degree of freedom to the overall design. Finally, a tactile sensory feedback system which uses force sensors and vibration motors was developed to enable sensation of the force inflicted on the hand for the user.

### Paper [89]
Title: SAP-CoPE: Social-Aware Planning using Cooperative Pose Estimation with Infrastructure Sensor Nodes
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.05727
Authors: Minghao Ning, Yufeng Yang, Shucheng Huang, Jiaming Zhong, Keqi Shu, Chen Sun, Ehsan Hashemi, Amir Khajepour
Abstract: Autonomous mobile robots must operate smoothly in human-populated indoor environments, where onboard-only sensing suffers from limited perception range and occlusions, and socially compliant motion planning must account for human psychological comfort zones. These factors complicate accurate recognition of human intentions and the generation of comfortable, social-aware trajectories. To address these challenges, we propose SAP-CoPE, an indoor navigation framework that integrates cooperative infrastructure sensing, human pose estimation, and a social-aware MPC planner. In the perception module, an uncertainty-aware optimization problem is formulated to estimate 3D human poses from camera observations, with optional incorporation of sparse LiDAR point-cloud measurements, while considering human joint coherence. In the planning module, the estimated human pose and orientation are used to construct a personal-space (PS) field within an MPC framework, enabling the robot to account for both physical safety and psychological comfort. Extensive real-world evaluations demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach in generating social-aware trajectories for autonomous systems.

### Paper [90]
Title: UniConFlow: A Unified Constrained Flow-Matching Framework for Certified Motion Planning
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02955
Authors: Zewen Yang, Xiaobing Dai, Dian Yu, Zhijun Li, Majid Khadiv, Sandra Hirche, Sami Haddadin
Abstract: Generative models have become increasingly powerful tools for robot motion generation, enabling flexible and multimodal trajectory generation across various tasks. Yet, most existing approaches remain limited in handling multiple types of constraints, such as collision avoidance, actuation limits, and dynamic consistency, which are typically addressed individually or heuristically. In this work, we propose UniConFlow, a unified constrained flow matching-based framework for trajectory generation that systematically incorporates both equality and inequality constraints. Moreover, UniConFlow introduces a novel prescribed-time zeroing function that shapes a time-varying guidance field during inference, allowing the generation process to adapt to varying system models and task requirements. Furthermore, to further address the computational challenges of long-horizon and high-dimensional trajectory generation, we propose two practical strategies for the terminal constraint enforcement and inference process: a violation-segment extraction protocol that precisely localizes and refines only the constraint-violating portions of trajectories, and a trajectory compression method that accelerates optimization in a reduced-dimensional space while preserving high-fidelity reconstruction after decoding. Empirical validation across three experiments, including a double inverted pendulum, a real-to-sim car racing task, and a sim-to-real manipulation task, demonstrates that UniConFlow outperforms state-of-the-art generative planners and conventional optimization baselines, achieving superior performance on certified motion planning metrics such as safety, kinodynamic consistency, and action feasibility. Project page is available at: https://uniconflow.github.io.

### Paper [91]
Title: Injecting Hallucinations in Autonomous Vehicles: A Component-Agnostic Safety Evaluation Framework
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.07749
Authors: Alexandre Moreira Nascimento, Gabriel Kenji Godoy Shimanuki, L\'ucio Flavio Vismari, Jo\~ao Batista Camargo Jr, Jorge Rady de Almeida Jr, Paulo Sergio Cugnasca, Anna Carolina Muller Queiroz, Jeremy Noah Bailenson
Abstract: Perception failures in autonomous vehicles (AV) remain a major safety concern because they can directly compromise decision-making and contribute to unsafe behavior. To study how these failures affect safety, researchers typically inject artificial faults into real-world or simulated components and observe the resulting outcomes. However, existing fault injection (FI) studies often target a single sensor within the machine perception (MP) module, resulting in siloed frameworks that are difficult to generalize or integrate into unified simulation environments. This work addresses that limitation by reframing perception failures as hallucinations, that is, false perceptions that distort an AV's situational awareness and may trigger unsafe control actions. Building on this concept, we propose a configurable, component-agnostic hallucination injection framework that induces six plausible hallucination types in an open-source simulation environment. A total of 18,356 simulations (8,695 baseline and 9,661 with hallucinations injected) were executed while AVs crossed an unsignalized intersection with transversal traffic. The results statistically validate the framework and quantify the impact of each hallucination type on collisions and on the minimum distance between the AV and the nearest vehicle, used as a continuous proxy for the safety margin. Certain hallucinations, especially missed detection, blind region, angular drift, phantom perception, and latency, significantly increase the odds of collision, while linear drift significantly reduces minimum distances without producing a statistically significant increase in collision odds in the evaluated scenario. The proposed framework provides a scalable and statistically grounded method for evaluating AV safety under perception distortions and supports future research on fault tolerance and resilient AV design.

### Paper [92]
Title: DeFM: Learning Foundation Representations from Depth for Robotics
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.18923
Authors: Manthan Patel, Jonas Frey, Mayank Mittal, Fan Yang, Alexander Hansson, Amir Bar, Cesar Cadena, Marco Hutter
Abstract: Depth sensors are widely deployed across robotic platforms, and advances in fast, high-fidelity depth simulation have enabled robotic policies trained on depth observations to achieve robust sim-to-real transfer for a wide range of tasks. Despite this, representation learning for depth modality remains underexplored compared to RGB, where large-scale foundation models now define the state of the art. To address this gap, we present DeFM, a self-supervised foundation model trained entirely on depth images for robotic applications. Using a DINO-style self-distillation objective on a curated dataset of 60M depth images, DeFM learns geometric and semantic representations that generalize to diverse environments, tasks, and sensors. To retain metric awareness across multiple scales, we introduce a novel input normalization strategy. We further distill DeFM into compact models suitable for resource-constrained robotic systems. When evaluated on depth-based classification, segmentation, navigation, locomotion, and manipulation benchmarks, DeFM achieves state-of-the-art performance and demonstrates strong generalization from simulation to real-world environments. We release all our pretrained models, which can be adopted off-the-shelf for depth-based robotic learning without task-specific fine-tuning. Webpage: https://de-fm.github.io/

### Paper [93]
Title: DiffMorphGrasp: A Diffusion Framework Conditioned on Hand Morphology for Cross-Embodiment Grasp Generation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.00915
Authors: Zhiyuan Wu, Xiangyu Zhang, Zhuo Chen, Jiankang Deng, Rolandos Alexandros Potamias, Shan Luo
Abstract: As robots are deployed across diverse environments, manipulation tasks require robot hands with different morphologies to satisfy varying task requirements, hardware capabilities, and cost constraints. However, transferring grasping policies learned on one robot hand to another with a different morphology remains a fundamental challenge due to variations in kinematic structures and degrees of freedom across embodiments. To address these limitations, we propose DiffMorphGrasp, the first diffusion-based framework that incorporates hand morphology information into the generative process for cross-embodiment dexterous grasping. The proposed approach maps grasps from diverse robotic hands into a reference hand pose, providing a common space for learning. Grasp generation is then continuously conditioned on structured representations of hand kinematics, encoded as graphs derived from hand configurations, together with object geometry. In addition, we introduce a loss function that computes joint-level errors over the hand kinematic structure to constrain the generated grasps during training. To evaluate DiffMorphGrasp, we construct a new benchmark for assessing grasp generation performance across robot hands with different morphologies. Extensive experiments demonstrate that DiffMorphGrasp achieves strong zero-shot generalization to unseen hand morphologies, enabling scalable cross-embodiment grasp deployment. Project page: https://georgewuzy.github.io/DiffMorphGrasp-website

### Paper [94]
Title: HumanX: Toward Agile and Generalizable Humanoid Interaction Skills from Human Videos
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.02473
Authors: Yinhuai Wang, Qihan Zhao, Yuen Fui Lau, Runyi Yu, Hok Wai Tsui, Qifeng Chen, Jingbo Wang, Jiangmiao Pang, Ping Tan
Abstract: Enabling humanoid robots to perform agile and adaptive interactions has long been a core challenge in robotics. Current approaches are bottlenecked by either the scarcity of realistic interaction data or the need for meticulous, task-specific reward engineering, which limits their scalability. To narrow this gap, we present HumanX, a unified framework that compiles human video into generalizable, real-world interaction skills for humanoids, without per-task design. HumanX integrates two co-designed components: XGen, an anchor-centric data generation pipeline that yields physically plausible robot interaction data from monocular video and augments it to create a diverse, scalable training dataset; and XMimic, an interaction-prioritized learning framework to achieve generalizable interaction skills from seconds of demonstration. Evaluated across five distinct domains--basketball, football, badminton, cargo pickup, and reactive fighting--HumanX successfully acquires 9 different skills and transfers them zero-shot to a physical Unitree G1 humanoid. The learned capabilities include complex maneuvers such as pump-fake turnaround fadeaway jumpshots without any external perception, as well as interactive tasks like sustained human-robot passing sequences over 10 consecutive cycles--learned from a single video demonstration. Our experiments show that HumanX achieves higher generalization success than prior methods, demonstrating a scalable pathway within a class of anchorable interactions for learning versatile, real-world robot interactive skills.

### Paper [95]
Title: USER: A Unified and Extensible System for Real-World Online Policy Learning in Embodied AI
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.07837
Authors: Hongzhi Zang, Shu'ang Yu, Hao Lin, Tianxing Zhou, Zefang Huang, Zhen Guo, Xin Xu, Jiakai Zhou, Yuze Sheng, Shizhe Zhang, Feng Gao, Wenhao Tang, Yufeng Yue, Quanlu Zhang, Xinlei Chen, Chao Yu, Yu Wang
Abstract: Online policy learning directly in the physical world is a promising yet challenging direction for embodied intelligence. Unlike simulation, real-world systems cannot be arbitrarily accelerated, cheaply reset, or massively replicated, suggesting that real-world policy learning is not merely an algorithmic problem, but inherently a systems problem. We present USER, a \underline{U}nified and extensible \underline{S}yst\underline{E}m for real-world online policy lea\underline{R}ning. On the systems side, USER introduces a hardware abstraction layer for unified robot management and an adaptive communication plane that enables efficient cloud-edge training. On the learning side, USER adopts a fully asynchronous training framework, designs a persistent and cache-aware replay buffer, and provides extensible abstractions for rewards, algorithms, and policies. Experiments in both simulation and the real world demonstrate that USER supports multi-robot coordination, heterogeneous manipulators, cloud-edge training with large models, and long-running asynchronous training. Together, these capabilities establish USER as a unified and extensible systems foundation for real-world online policy learning.

### Paper [96]
Title: Any House Any Task: Scalable Long-Horizon Planning for Abstract Human Tasks
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.12244
Authors: Zhihong Liu, Yang Li, Rengming Huang, Cewu Lu, Panpan Cai
Abstract: Open world language conditioned task planning is crucial for robots operating in large-scale household environments. While many recent works attempt to address this problem using Large Language Models (LLMs) via prompting or training, a key challenge remains scalability. Performance often degrades rapidly with increasing environment size, plan length, instruction ambiguity, and constraint complexity. In this work, we propose Any House Any Task (AHAT), a household task planner optimized for long-horizon planning in large environments given ambiguous human instructions. At its core, AHAT utilizes an LLM trained to map task instructions and textual scene graphs into grounded subgoals defined in the Planning Domain Definition Language (PDDL). These subgoals are subsequently solved to generate feasible and optimal long-horizon plans through explicit symbolic reasoning. To enhance the model's ability to decompose complex and ambiguous intentions, we introduce TGPO, a novel reinforcement learning algorithm that integrates external correction of intermediate reasoning traces into Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). Experiments demonstrate that AHAT achieves significant performance gains over state-of-the-art prompting, planning, and learning methods, particularly in human-style household tasks characterized by brief instructions but requiring complex execution plans.

### Paper [97]
Title: Infinite-Dimensional Closed-Loop Inverse Kinematics for Soft Robots via Neural Operators
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.18655
Authors: Carina Veil, Moritz Flaschel, Ellen Kuhl, Cosimo Della Santina
Abstract: For fully actuated rigid robots, kinematic inversion is a purely geometric problem, efficiently solved by closed-loop inverse kinematics (CLIK) schemes that compute joint velocities to position the robot body in space. For underactuated soft robots, however, not all configurations are attainable through control action, making kinematic inversion extremely ill-posed. Extensions of CLIK address this by introducing end-to-end mappings from actuation to task space for the controller to operate on, but typically assume finite dimensions of the underlying virtual configuration space.
